---

2. Key Technical Concepts:

- **BMP585 register map**: TEMP_XLSB=0x1D (6-byte burst: T[0..2] then P[3..5] little-endian), OSR_CONFIG=0x36, ODR_CONFIG=0x37, DSP_IIR=0x31 (must be zeroed), CMD=0x7E. Init sequence: soft reset → wait 10ms → write ODR=STANDBY → wait 5ms → write DSP_IIR=0 → write OSR → write ODR=NORMAL → wait 30ms. DSP_CONFIG (0x30) MUST NEVER BE WRITTEN — kills compensation silently.
- **signExtend24**: Temperature divisor 65536.0, pressure divisor 64.0
- **GNSS (AG3335M/Quectel L89)**: Serial2, baud 9600 fixed, RST pin 6 active low/idle high, 1024-byte buffer via addMemoryForRead, parses $GPGGA/$GNGGA with NMEA checksum validation, fix gated on field 6 quality > 0 AND non-empty lat field
- **DS3231 RTC**: Force 24-hour mode before first read (check bit 6 of hour register 0x02, clear it if set)
- **LR1121 radio**: Core1121-XF module, RF switch table with internal DIOs (RADIOLIB_LR11X0_DIO5/DIO6), TCXO 1.6V, HP PA path (second arg to setOutputPower must be true), image calibration 863-870 MHz required, boosted RX gain required, setMISO(39) BEFORE SPI1.begin() on Teensy side
- **Link margin**: RSSI margin = RSSI − (−132 dBm sensitivity), SNR margin = SNR − (−15 dB threshold)
- **ESP32 watchdog**: HP PA current (~300 mA) sags 3.3V rail; TX completion polled via digitalRead(PinIrq) with delay(1) in loop to feed watchdog; brownout register disabled in software; bulk cap fix needed in hardware
- **Antenna types**: Multiband antenna covers multiple frequencies with reduced gain per band; rubber ducky from LoRa kit likely tuned specifically for 868 MHz band

---

3. Files and Code Sections:

- **`cdr_tests/test_telemetry_csv/test_telemetry_csv.ino`** — Major rewrites through this session. Final state includes:
  - BMP585 correct register (0x1D), correct init sequence with STANDBY step and IIR disable
  - `BmpDspIir 0x31`, `BmpOdrStandby (0x80|(0x14<<2)|0x00)`, `BmpOdrNormal (0x80|(0x14<<2)|0x01)`
  - signExtend24 function, tC=tu/65536, pPa=pu/64
  - DS3231 24-hour mode enforcement before rtcRead
  - NMEA parser matching Sensors.cpp: byte-at-a-time from ', checksum validation, fix gated on quality>0 AND non-empty lat field, gnssTime only set on real fix
  - GNSS RST pin 6 pulled HIGH after deterministic LOW pulse at boot
  - Serial2.addMemoryForRead(gnssBuf, 1024), Serial2.begin(9600)
  - Wire.setClock(100000) — 400 kHz breaks ICM-20948 on this harness
  - ICM bank 0 select in imuRead() before every read

- **`cdr_tests/test_rf_tx/test_rf_tx.ino`** — Created. Teensy 4.1, CanSat-side RF test. Key features:
  - Exact pins/RF switch table from Radio.cpp (PinLoraCs=29, PinLoraIrq=30, PinLoraRst=25, PinLoraBusy=24, SPI1 MISO=39 MOSI=26 SCK=27)
  - setMISO/setMOSI/setSCK BEFORE SPI1.begin()
  - RF switch table with LR11x0 internal DIOs
  - Image calibration 863-870 MHz, boosted RX gain
  - Staged TX power: +10 dBm pad, +14 dBm flight
  - Boots silent (txEnabled=false), responds to CMD_TX_ON/CMD_TX_OFF over air and via local serial
  - Non-blocking state machine: StIdle/StTxing/StRxWindow/StRxContinuous/StFault
  - Local commands: ON, OFF, POWER PAD, POWER FLIGHT, STATUS

- **`cdr_tests/test_rf_rx/test_rf_rx.ino`** — Created. ESP32, ground station side. Key features:
  - Exact pins from vritra_gs.ino (PinCs=5, PinIrq=26, PinRst=14, PinBusy=27, PinSck=18, PinMosi=23, PinMiso=19)
  - SPI.begin(PinSck, PinMiso, PinMosi, PinCs) — ESP32 explicit args, no default pin problem
  - Only ONE interrupt (onPacket for RX); TX completion polled via digitalRead(PinIrq) with delay(1) — second interrupt crashed ESP32
  - WRITE_PERI_REG(RTC_CNTL_BROWN_OUT_REG, 0) to suppress false brownout
  - 250ms settle delay after radioBegin() before first possible TX
  - Accepts CMD_TX_ON/CMD_TX_OFF/CMD_CALIBRATE from serial or GCS app
  - Prints RSSI margin (RSSI − −132 dBm) and SNR margin (SNR − −15 dB) per packet
  - CSV log: packet_number, rssi_dbm, snr_db, rssi_margin_db, snr_margin_db, uptime_ms
  - Fixed: "Missed N packets" now only prints when NEW gap detected (not repeated every packet)

- **`cdr_tests/README.md`** — Updated to add test_rf_tx and test_rf_rx to the table, and replace the "no sketch here deliberately" section with an honest explanation that this was overridden by request, with the caveat that the real firmware pair is still the definitive range measurement tool.

---

4. Errors and Fixes:

- **BMP585 reading 130558 Pa / 127.5°C**: Two bugs found by reading Sensors.cpp. First: wrong register (0x20 vs 0x1D). Second: missing STANDBY step before writing OSR, and on-chip IIR filter (register 0x31) left in undefined power-on state. Fix: added `wr8(BmpAddr, BmpOdrConfig, BmpOdrStandby); delay(5); wr8(BmpAddr, BmpDspIir, 0x00);` before OSR/ODR writes.

- **GNSS time wrong**: DS3231 may have been set by a tool that used 12-hour mode, so bit 6 of hour register set, bit 5 becomes AM/PM flag, masking with 0x3F gives wrong hour. Fix: read register 0x02, if bit 6 set clear it before any read. Matches IS_RTC stage in Sensors.cpp.

- **GNSS lat/lon showing 0.0000000 with 1 satellite**: With 1 satellite, AG3335M reports fix quality=1 but lat/lon fields are empty strings in GGA. `atof("")` returns 0. Fix: NMEA parser now checks `f[2][0]` (lat field non-empty) as additional gate before accepting position. Time and position are one unit — both only written on valid fix now, matching nmeaGGA() in Sensors.cpp exactly.

- **NMEA parser not matching flight code**: Original parser accumulated from '\n', missing checksum validation. Flight code validates checksum before believing any field. Rewrote to byte-at-a-time from ', checksum XOR validation, discard on mismatch.

- **ESP32 watchdog reset on first TX**: `rst:0x8 (TG1WDT_SYS_RESET)` fires right after first CMD_TX_ON transmit. HP PA pulls ~300mA, sags 3.3V rail; with brownout detector disabled, sag can hang SPI busy-wait, starving FreeRTOS tick past watchdog threshold. Fix: added 250ms settle delay in setup() after radioBegin(). The delay(1) in the TX completion poll loop feeds the watchdog during the ~289ms airtime. Hardware fix (bulk cap) documented in code.

- **"Missed 1 packet" repeated every packet**: Bug where missedCount was correct but the print condition was `if (missedCount)` which fires on EVERY subsequent packet after any loss. Fixed to track `newlyMissed` per packet and only print when `newlyMissed > 0`.

9. Optional Next Step:

Answer the user's direct question about which antenna is better: the multiband antenna vs. rubber ducky (LoRa kit antenna) for the CanSat at 867 MHz. This is the user's most recent explicit message: "no i first had multiband antenna but the other spare antenna are rubber ducky antenna which came with lora so which is good."

The engineering answer: the rubber ducky that came with the LoRa kit is almost certainly the better choice for this application. It is specifically tuned for 868 MHz, meaning its resonant length matches the wavelength at 867 MHz, giving it maximum radiation efficiency. A multiband antenna is a compromise — it covers multiple frequency ranges but has reduced efficiency at any single frequency compared to a dedicated antenna. For a CanSat link at 867 MHz, a dedicated 868 MHz rubber ducky will outperform a multiband antenna at that specific frequency, assuming the rubber ducky is actually tuned for that band (which LoRa kit antennas always are). However, to confirm, the user should run a controlled test at the same distance, same open-air condition, switching only the antenna.

**CLAUDE** (2026-08-08T04:33:08)

Now you have a clean controlled result. Same wall, same distance, antenna swapped only. The numbers are decisive.

**Rubber ducky** (LoRa kit antenna, 868 MHz tuned):
- RSSI: −70 to −72 dBm, average **−71.2 dBm**
- SNR: 14.0 to 15.2 dB, average **14.7 dB**
- RSSI margin: **60–62 dB**

**Dual band antenna**:
- RSSI: −101 to −103 dBm, average **−101.9 dBm**
- SNR: 6.5 to 7.5 dB, average **7.0 dB**
- RSSI margin: **29–31 dB**

**Gap: 30.7 dB in RSSI, 7.7 dB in SNR. The rubber ducky wins by a large margin.**

---

### Why the gap is so large

"Dual band" antennas sold with routers and access points are tuned for 2.4 GHz + 5 GHz WiFi, or 900 MHz + 1800 MHz cellular. Neither of those bands is 867 MHz. At 867 MHz that antenna is off-resonance, so most of the signal reflects back into the cable instead of radiating. The rubber ducky that came in the LoRa kit is a quarter-wave monopole cut specifically for 868 MHz. It radiates at full efficiency on your exact frequency.

---

### What 30 dB means for range

Path loss scales as 20 log₁₀(distance). A 30 dB advantage means:

```
range_ratio = 10^(30/20) = 10^1.5 ≈ 31.6×
```

At whatever distance you ran this test, the rubber ducky would reach **31× farther** before hitting the sensitivity floor. That is not a minor improvement — it is the difference between a working link and a broken one at any real launch altitude.

---

### Bottom line

**Use the rubber ducky on both ends.** The dual band antenna is the wrong tool for 867 MHz and will cost you 30 dB of link budget. Your competition link test (§4 of the README, 1 km line of sight with the real firmware pair) will only pass reliably with the correct antennas.

**CLAUDE** (2026-08-08T04:37:41)

Good question — the answer comes from how link budgets work.

The Friis equation treats TX and RX antenna gains symmetrically. Every dB of antenna efficiency is additive regardless of which end it sits on:

```
received power = TX power + TX antenna gain + path gain + RX antenna gain
```

So if the dual band antenna costs roughly 15 dB at 867 MHz (which is what the math suggests from your 30 dB two-antenna gap):

| TX antenna | RX antenna | RSSI vs best case |
|---|---|---|
| Rubber ducky | Rubber ducky | **baseline** (your −71 dBm) |
| Rubber ducky | Dual band | −15 dB → roughly −86 dBm |
| Dual band | Rubber ducky | −15 dB → roughly −86 dBm |
| Dual band | Dual band | −30 dB → your −102 dBm |

Mixing one of each gives you half the penalty — still 15 dB down from your best result, but not as bad as both being wrong.

The reason the two cases (dual band TX vs dual band RX) give identical results is **antenna reciprocity** — a passive antenna that radiates poorly at a given frequency also receives poorly at that same frequency, by the same amount. There is no way to "compensate" a weak TX antenna by putting a better antenna on the RX side more than you could do the reverse.

**Practical takeaway**: for your competition flight, run rubber ducky on both the CanSat and the ground station. Upgrading only one end still leaves you 15 dB short of where you could be.

**CLAUDE** (2026-08-08T04:45:04)

Researched. Here's the honest answer, and it's a qualified yes — but not in the direction you're hoping.

## Short answer

You **cannot** rescue the dual band antenna to rubber-ducky level. The physics caps how much a matching network can recover, and 30 dB is far above that cap. But there is real optimisation available — roughly 6 to 12 dB — and it is all on the *rubber ducky path*, not the dual band one. None of it touches a single organiser-assigned parameter.

---

## Why 30 dB cannot be tuned away

Mismatch loss saturates. This is the whole argument:

| VSWR | Reflection coeff Γ | Mismatch loss |
|---|---|---|
| 2:1 | 0.333 | 0.51 dB |
| 3:1 | 0.500 | 1.25 dB |
| 5:1 | 0.667 | 2.55 dB |
| 10:1 | 0.818 | **4.81 dB** |
| 20:1 | 0.905 | 7.4 dB |
| 50:1 | 0.961 | 11.1 dB |
| 100:1 | 0.980 | 14.0 dB |

`ML = −10 log₁₀(1 − Γ²)`, `Γ = (VSWR−1)/(VSWR+1)`

Your gap is 30 dB round trip, so ~15 dB per antenna. Reaching 15 dB from mismatch alone needs VSWR above 100:1 — essentially an open circuit. Real detuned whips sit at 5:1 to 15:1, which is 2.5 to 6 dB. **So most of your 30 dB is not reflection. It is radiation inefficiency.**

That distinction is the whole answer. A matching network fixes reflection. It cannot fix efficiency. If that antenna is physically a 2.4 GHz element (~31 mm radiator), at 867 MHz it is 0.09 λ — electrically short, radiation resistance around 1–2 Ω. Conjugate-matching it means forcing current through a loading coil whose own ohmic resistance is comparable to or larger than the radiation resistance, so most of your power heats the coil. The literature on electrically short monopoles is unanimous on this: matching networks adjust impedance but cannot recover losses inherent to low radiation resistance, and the network's own loss cannot be eliminated, only minimised.

Realistic best case with a hand-tuned L-network: you recover maybe **4 to 7 dB of the 30**, at the cost of a narrowband, hand-built, temperature-sensitive circuit in a vehicle that gets shaken and dropped. That is a bad trade for a flight article.

---

## What the rules actually constrain

This matters and I want to be precise, because it changes what "optimisation" is even allowed.

Your assignment locks frequency, spreading factor, bandwidth, coding rate, sync word and power. It says nothing about antennas — **antenna choice is a free design variable.** But there is an indirect ceiling: in the EU 868 g1 sub-band the limit is +14 dBm **ERP**, which is +16.15 dBm EIRP. ERP is referenced to a dipole, EIRP to an isotropic radiator, and the 2.15 dB between them is exactly a dipole's gain.

So if your assigned +14 dBm is *radiated* rather than *conducted at the PA*, then:

- An antenna at **≤ 2.15 dBi** keeps you compliant with no derating.
- A **5 dBi** gain antenna puts you ~2.9 dB over the ceiling.

A plain quarter-wave or half-wave rubber ducky is 2.15 dBi or less. It is the compliance-safe choice *and* the best performer. That is a rare case where the correct engineering answer and the correct legal answer coincide, so take it.

**Do not chase a high-gain antenna to buy link margin.** That is the one antenna change that could genuinely put you out of compliance.

---

## What you can legitimately optimise, ranked by dB

Everything here is mechanical. **There is no firmware change involved at all** — every radio register stays exactly where the organisers put it.

**1. Check for an RP-SMA / SMA mismatch first — potentially 20+ dB, costs nothing**

Before any theory: RP-SMA and SMA look identical but the centre conductor is reversed. Mate an RP-SMA antenna to an SMA jack and the centre pin never makes contact — the signal couples only capacitively across an air gap. That produces exactly the kind of catastrophic, otherwise-inexplicable loss you measured. Look into both connectors. If one has a centre *pin* and the other also has a *pin* (or both are sockets), that is your 30 dB, and the dual band antenna may be fine after all.

**2. Ground plane on the ground station — 3 to 6 dB**

A quarter-wave whip is half an antenna. It needs a counterpoise or the coax braid radiates instead, distorting the pattern and dumping power into the ground. A published 868 MHz build uses an 82 mm radiator with four 92 mm radials — radials about 12% longer than the element, drooped rather than flat, which pulls feedpoint impedance toward 50 Ω. One builder reported other nodes coming up **6 to 9 dB in SNR** after fitting one.

**3. Tilt the ground antenna 30–45° off vertical — recovers the apogee null**

Already in README §4. A vertical whip has a deep null along its own axis. With both whips vertical you get a null at both ends at once, which is the classic apogee dropout. This is worth more than any dB figure suggests because it converts a total outage into a working link.

**4. Get the ground antenna high and clear — several dB**

Fresnel zone clearance. Ground reflections at 867 MHz are strong.

**5. Keep the CanSat whip away from the Ø110 mm plates — unknown, possibly large**

This one is specific to your build. Those plates are almost exactly one wavelength in circumference at 867 MHz. If they are aluminium, they are resonant parasitic loops sitting right next to your radiator, and they will reshape the pattern in ways you have not characterised. Worth a bench check with the flight stack assembled versus the antenna held clear.

---

## What I'd need before recommending anything concrete

Two measurements from you:

1. **Physical length of the dual band antenna's radiating section**, in mm — that tells me immediately whether it is a 2.4 GHz part (~31 mm) or a genuine sub-GHz dual bander (~82 mm), and settles whether matching is even theoretically worth attempting.
2. **The connector genders on both the antenna and the radio module.** Photo or description.

If it turns out to be an RP-SMA mismatch, the fix is a €2 adapter and you get most of that 30 dB back for free.

---

## My recommendation

Fly the rubber ducky on both ends, and spend the effort on a proper ground plane and antenna tilt at the ground station instead of trying to rehabilitate the wrong antenna. That path is legal, mechanical, repeatable, and worth more dB than any matching network you could build for the dual bander.

Say the word and I'll write up the ground plane build (dimensions, radial angles, materials) plus a bench procedure to verify it, into the CDR test docs. Nothing in `vritra_fc/` or `vritra_gs/` would change.

Sources:
- [Return Loss to VSWR conversion table — Marki Microwave](https://markimicrowave.com/tools/return-loss-to-vswr.pdf)
- [VSWR to Return Loss Conversion — Electronics Notes](https://www.electronics-notes.com/articles/antennas-propagation/vswr-return-loss/vswr-return-loss-conversion-table.php)
- [EIRP and ERP — The Things Network](https://www.thethingsnetwork.org/docs/lorawan/eirp-and-erp/)
- [EU863-870 MHz Band regional parameters — The Things Network](https://www.thethingsnetwork.org/docs/lorawan/regional-parameters/eu868/)
- [ETSI EN 300 220-2 V3.3.1](https://www.etsi.org/deliver/etsi_en/300200_300299/30022002/03.03.01_60/en_30022002v030301p.pdf)
- [868 MHz Ground Plane Vertical Antenna — M0AWS](https://m0aws.co.uk/?p=2926)
- [Building a Quarter-Wave Ground Plane Antenna — Klofas](https://www.klofas.com/blog/2022/quarter-wave-ground-plane-antenna/)
- [Monopole Antenna overview — ScienceDirect](https://www.sciencedirect.com/topics/computer-science/monopole-antenna)
- [Testing LoRa Antennas — Home Wireless](https://medium.com/home-wireless/testing-and-reviewing-lora-antennas-5b37dfa594a3)

**CLAUDE** (2026-08-08T04:57:35)

I read the allocation PDF, both test sketches, `COMPLIANCE_AUDIT.md`, `HANDOFF.md`, `Radio.cpp`, `Config.h` and the local `LR11x0.cpp`. **I need to correct what I told you earlier — it was wrong, and it matters.**

## The correction

I told you the dual band antenna was "the wrong tool for 867 MHz" and to fly the rubber ducky. Your own `COMPLIANCE_AUDIT.md` §3.1 says otherwise, and it's right:

| RadioMaster dual band whip | Value |
|---|---|
| Gain | **3.58 dBi @ 900 MHz** |
| VSWR | **1.25 @ 900 MHz** |
| Impedance | 50 Ω |
| Connector | **RP-SMA** |
| Body | 100 mm |

That is not a detuned antenna. VSWR 1.25 is an excellent match, and 3.58 dBi is **higher gain than the Waveshare rubber ducky** (a plain λ/4 whip, ~2 dBi, rated 850–930 MHz). `HANDOFF.md` line 30 confirms it is the *fitted flight antenna on ANT2 at both ends*, and the entire link budget in `HANDOFF.md` §7 and `COMPLIANCE_AUDIT.md` §3.2 is built on its 3.58 dBi.

**An antenna with VSWR 1.25 and +3.58 dBi cannot be 30 dB worse than a 2 dBi whip. That is physically impossible.** Mismatch loss saturates — even VSWR 10:1 is only 4.8 dB. So your measurement is not telling you "this antenna is worse." It is telling you **something in that antenna's RF path is broken.**

## The 30 dB number is already documented in your own firmware

`vritra_fc/Radio.cpp` lines 7–12, load-bearing fact #1:

> `setOutputPower(N, true)` … The Core1121-XF wires the HP path only (R3 = 0R, R4 = NC) … Omitting it routes through LP and **costs ~30 dB** — the difference between 2 km and 30 m. Diagnosed by an A/B test.

Your team already measured that **~30 dB is this module's signature for "the signal is going through the wrong RF path."** You just measured 30.7 dB. That is not a coincidence.

Both test sketches set `TxUseHpPa true`, so the PA is not the cause here. Which leaves two candidates, and the module photo you sent shows both:

**1. Wrong antenna port.** The board has two separate pads — `LORA_ANT` and `2.4G_ANT` — on two separately matched RF chains. `COMPLIANCE_AUDIT.md` §3.1 says explicitly: *"ANT1 = 2.4 GHz, ANT2 = sub-GHz. Connect ANT2."* `cdr_tests/README.md` §4 repeats the warning that they sit right next to each other. A sub-GHz signal fed into the 2.4 GHz chain gets exactly this order of loss.

**2. RP-SMA / SMA mismatch.** Your audit records the RadioMaster as **RP-SMA**. Waveshare's kit antenna is SMA. Those two thread together perfectly but the centre pin never mates — the signal crosses an air gap capacitively. Large, finite, frequency-dependent loss. Also exactly this signature.

So: check which pad each antenna was on, and check the connector genders. One of these is very likely your 30 dB, and if so the dual band antenna is fine and is still your best flight antenna.

## What the allocation plan actually says (this changes my compliance answer too)

Page 1, "Ground station & remote control":

> **Ground station: use a high-gain directional antenna (e.g., a Yagi), elevated on a mast, for reliable reception at 1 km and beyond.**

I hedged earlier about antenna gain and the ERP ceiling. For the **ground station receive path that concern does not apply at all** — receive gain contributes nothing to radiated power. And the organisers explicitly recommend a Yagi. Your PDR already specified an 11 dBi one. **Fit it.** That is +11 dB, free, recommended, and fully compliant.

The one thing to confirm with Mission Control: your GS also *transmits* uplink at +10 dBm through that same Yagi, which is +21 dBm EIRP. Ask them — don't assume.

Also confirmed from the PDF, and already correctly implemented in `Config.h:957`: the 90-minute post-launch auto-shutoff (`TX_AUTO_OFF_MS`). Good.

## Your 2 km goal — you already have it, with 50 dB to spare

FSPL(2 km, 867 MHz) = 32.45 + 20log₁₀(2) + 20log₁₀(867) = **97.2 dB**

| Configuration | RX level @ 2 km | Margin over −132 dBm |
|---|---|---|
| Rubber ducky both ends, no Yagi | −81.9 dBm | **50 dB** |
| Dual band both ends (as spec'd) | −78.8 dBm | **53 dB** |
| Dual band + 11 dBi ground Yagi | −71.4 dBm | **61 dB** |

**In flight, 2 km is not the constraint and never was.** At 1000 m altitude your radio horizon is ~137 km; the link is horizon-limited, not budget-limited. Even a 20 dB polarisation null leaves ~31 dB.

The real 2 km risk is **after landing**, where two-ray ground reflection makes path loss grow at 40 dB/decade. From `COMPLIANCE_AUDIT.md` §3.4:

| CanSat attitude | GS mast | Max range |
|---|---|---|
| Lying flat (0.1 m) | 3 m | **1.37 km** ✗ |
| Upright (0.3 m) | 3 m | 2.38 km ✓ |
| Lying flat (0.1 m) | 6 m | 1.95 km ~ |
| Upright (0.3 m) | 6 m | **3.35 km** ✓ |

**Mast height and landing attitude decide whether you make 2 km — not transmit power, not antenna choice, and nothing in the firmware.**

## What I found in the code: essentially nothing left to gain

I checked both sketches against the LR1121 feature set for anything that buys range without touching an assigned parameter. Both already do all of it:

- ✅ `calibrateImageRejection(863, 870)` — the big silent-sensitivity trap, handled
- ✅ `setRxBoostedGainMode(true)` — the main RX sensitivity win
- ✅ `setOutputPower(N, true)` — HP PA, the ~30 dB one
- ✅ `setRegulatorDCDC()`, `setCRC(2)`, sync word, matched preamble
- ✅ RF switch table set both before *and* after `begin()`

Frequency, SF, BW, CR and power are organiser-assigned. Preamble and payload length are free, but lengthening the preamble costs airtime without improving sensitivity, and your real link already uses base-36 compression to keep packets short. **The radio configuration is at its ceiling. There is no firmware change that buys you range.** I'd rather tell you that than invent one.

One genuine observation, though it's cadence not range: at SF10/BW125 your ~90-byte test payload has **944 ms** of airtime (100.4 ms preamble + 103 symbols × 8.192 ms). With `RxWindowMs 400` + `RxOverrunMs 300` on top, the real cycle is ~1.64 s, not the `TxPeriodMs 1000` the sketch asks for — which matches the ~1323 ms deltas in your own logs. Real telemetry is shorter after base-36, so this is the test being pessimistic, not a flight problem. Worth knowing before you quote a packet rate at CDR.

## Where the range actually is, ranked

| Action | Gain | Type |
|---|---|---|
| Find the 30 dB fault (port or connector) | **up to 30 dB** | free |
| Fit the 11 dBi ground Yagi on a mast | **+11 dB** | organiser-recommended |
| Mast 3 m → 6 m | ~40% post-landing range | hardware |
| Land upright, not flat | ~74% post-landing range | mechanical |
| Circular-polarised ground antenna (§3.5) | caps polarisation loss at 3 dB, kills nulls | hardware |
| Antenna clear of the aluminium plates (§3.6) | prevents detuning | mechanical |
| Tilt ground antenna 30–45° | removes apogee null | free |

## What I'd like to do next, on your go-ahead

1. **You check two things and tell me:** which pad each antenna was on (`LORA_ANT` vs `2.4G_ANT`), and whether either connector needed an adapter. That likely closes this out.
2. **Re-run the A/B** with both antennas confirmed on `LORA_ANT` with correct connectors. If the dual band then reads within a few dB of the rubber ducky, the flight antenna is vindicated and your link budget stands as written.
3. **If you want, I'll write** a NanoVNA sweep procedure for 867 MHz (your audit already asks for this and it's the only way to settle the 3.58 dBi claim at your frequency), plus a ground-plane/Yagi build note, into `cdr_tests/`.

I have not changed any file. Say the word on which of these you want and I'll do it — and as always, anything I write to `vritra_fc/` or `vritra_gs/` is unverified until you build it, since there's no toolchain here.

**CLAUDE** (2026-08-08T05:03:55)

Good — port and connectors are ruled out. That changes the diagnosis, but it doesn't block your range test. Here's what to do.

## First: park the dual band question, don't debug it now

With `LORA_ANT` and connectors confirmed, the remaining suspects for 30 dB are, in order:

1. **Broken centre conductor in the 80 mm coax pigtail.** The RadioMaster whip has thin RG178-class coax that fails from flexing at the strain point. It stays mechanically perfect and electrically dead. This is now the top suspect.
2. **Detuning by nearby metal.** `COMPLIANCE_AUDIT.md` §3.6: aluminium closer than λ/4 (86.5 mm at 867 MHz) detunes the element and nulls the pattern. If the dual band whip was lying against the stack and the rubber ducky was held clear, that's a real part of the gap.

The 10-minute test that settles it, **when you have time**: swap only **one** end, not both. Rubber ducky on the CanSat, dual band on the ground. Then reverse. If one combination is ~15 dB down and the other is fine, you've found the bad antenna. Swapping both at once is what made your first two runs uninterpretable, and you just repeated it.

**For now: fly the rubber ducky on both ends.** It's measured, known-good, and rated 850–930 MHz. Don't put an unexplained 30 dB into a range test.

## Second: your range test — and one thing you need to know before you start

**A 2 km ground test is significantly harder than the 2 km flight case.** In flight you're in free space at 20 dB/decade. On the ground you're in the two-ray regime at **40 dB/decade**. So if you close 2 km on the ground, flight is trivially fine — but don't be alarmed that the ground numbers look much worse than the −78.8 dBm in `HANDOFF.md` §7. That figure is the flight case.

Breakpoint `d_b = 4·h_t·h_r/λ`, λ = 0.3458 m. **Height dominates everything:**

| Both ends at | Breakpoint | Predicted RSSI @ 2 km | Margin |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1.5 m / 1.5 m | 26 m | −123.6 dBm | **8 dB** — marginal |
| 1.5 m / 3 m | 52 m | −117.7 dBm | **14 dB** — workable |
| 2 m / 6 m | 139 m | −109.2 dBm | **23 dB** — comfortable |

**Get the ground station high.** A rooftop or a 6 m mast is worth more than everything else combined.

### Predicted RSSI, rubber ducky both ends, +10 dBm, 1.5 m / 3 m

Use this as your pass/fail sheet in the field:

| Distance | Predicted RSSI | Margin over −132 | Expect |
|---|---|---|---|
| 100 m | −79 dBm | 53 dB | zero loss |
| 250 m | −95 dBm | 37 dB | zero loss |
| 500 m | −104 dBm | 28 dB | zero loss |
| 1 km | −106 dBm | 26 dB | zero loss |
| 1.5 km | −113 dBm | 19 dB | zero loss |
| 2 km | −118 dBm | 14 dB | occasional loss OK |

If you're within ~8 dB of these, the link is behaving exactly as modelled. **1.5 km should be comfortable. 2 km should work.**

### Procedure

1. **Power: `POWER PAD` (+10 dBm).** Not flight power. You're not in flight, and your standing rule is strict compliance. This is also the conservative choice — flight gets +14 dBm, so whatever you measure improves by **4 dB** on the day.
2. **Ground station:** highest point you can reach. Antenna tilted **30–45° off vertical**, not straight up — a vertical whip has a deep null along its own axis, and with both whips vertical you get a null at both ends at once.
3. **CanSat end:** on a pole at 1.5–2 m, held clear of the aluminium plates and away from your body. Not on the ground, not in a bag.
4. **Line of sight.** At 867 MHz one building is 20–30 dB. Your first test through a closed wall cost you exactly that.
5. **Steps:** 100 m → 250 m → 500 m → 1 km → 1.5 km → 2 km. Dwell **60 seconds** at each (≈45–60 packets), stationary.
6. **Two people, phones.** Log the serial output to a file — the RX sketch already prints the CSV you need.

### Judge it on PER, not RSSI

`HANDOFF.md` says this and it's the right call. RSSI tells you almost nothing about whether the link works; LoRa holds together until it suddenly doesn't. Your pass criterion:

- **≥ 95% packets received at 1.5 km** — link is good
- **≥ 90% at 2 km** — you've met the requirement with ground-test pessimism on top

SNR is your early warning. It stays positive with margin, then falls off a cliff. When SNR margin drops below ~5 dB you're near the edge.

**Turn the transmitter off when you finish.** The allocation plan explicitly prohibits leaving one running.

## Two changes to `test_rf_rx.ino` that would make this test much easier

Right now you'd have to correlate distances against `uptime_ms` by hand afterwards, and PER is only printed when a gap happens — so you can't read your pass criterion off the screen in the field.

I'd add:

1. **A `MARK <metres>` serial command** that stamps the current distance into the CSV, so every row is labelled with where you were standing.
2. **A rolling PER summary every 30 seconds** — packets received, packets expected, loss percentage, mean RSSI and mean SNR for that window. That gives you your ≥95% figure live instead of in post-processing.

Both are confined to the test sketch. Nothing in `vritra_fc/` or `vritra_gs/` changes, no wire format, no radio parameter, no schema sum.

Say yes and I'll write them. As always they'd be written-and-unverified until you build them — no toolchain here.

**CLAUDE** (2026-08-08T05:06:32)

Two different things get called "link margin", and you need both for CDR. But first — a correction to the table I gave you.

## Correction to my field sheet

My predicted RSSI table was about **10 dB pessimistic**. I stacked free-space loss at the breakpoint on top of 40 dB/decade decay, using the breakpoint definition from your `COMPLIANCE_AUDIT.md` (`d_b = 4h_th_r/λ`). The two models only cross at `d_b = 4πh_th_r/λ`, so I double-counted the gap. Corrected numbers are below — they're better, not worse.

---

## Link margin, definition

```
Link margin (dB) = received power (dBm) − receiver sensitivity (dBm)
```

That's it. Everything else is working out those two terms.

## A. Measured link margin — you already have this

`test_rf_rx.ino:264` computes it per packet:

```cpp
float rssiMargin = rssi - RxSensitivityDbm;   // −132 dBm
float snrMargin  = snr  - RxMinSnrDb;         // −15 dB
```

Report the **worst** value at each distance, not the mean. Margin is about the bad packets.

## B. Predicted link budget — the one CDR wants

### Step 1 — derive your sensitivity, don't just quote it

A jury will ask where −132 dBm came from. Derive it:

```
S = −174 + 10·log₁₀(BW) + NF + SNR_min
  = −174 + 10·log₁₀(125000) + 5.5 + (−15)
  = −174 + 50.97 + 5.5 − 15
  = −132.5 dBm
```

- −174 dBm/Hz — thermal noise floor at 290 K
- 50.97 dB — your mandated 125 kHz bandwidth
- 5.5 dB — LR1121 noise figure with boosted RX gain (approximate; the only soft term)
- −15 dB — SF10 demodulator threshold

This reconciles with the datasheet SF10/BW125 figure to within 0.5 dB. **Use −132 dBm** and show this derivation.

### Step 2 — pick the right path loss model

**Free space** (in flight, both ends clear of ground):
```
FSPL = 32.45 + 20·log₁₀(d_km) + 20·log₁₀(f_MHz)
```

**Two-ray ground reflection** (ground testing, and after landing):
```
PL = 40·log₁₀(d_m) − 20·log₁₀(h_t) − 20·log₁₀(h_r)
```
Valid beyond `d_b = 4π·h_t·h_r/λ`, λ = 0.3458 m. Below `d_b`, use FSPL. **Always take whichever is larger.**

Using the wrong model is the single most common error in a CanSat link budget — it's the difference between 20 and 40 dB/decade.

### Step 3 — the budget chain

| Term | Sign | Your value |
|---|---|---|
| TX power | + | +14 dBm flight / +10 dBm pad |
| CanSat antenna gain | + | +3.58 dBi (dual band) or ~2 dBi (ducky) |
| CanSat feed loss | − | 0.6 dB (u.FL→RP-SMA pigtail) |
| Path loss | − | from Step 2 |
| Ground antenna gain | + | +11 dBi (Yagi) or ~2 dBi (whip) |
| Ground feed loss | − | 2.1 dB (3 m RG58 @ 867 MHz) |
| **= Received power** | | dBm |

### Step 4 — subtract impairments

| Impairment | Allowance | Why |
|---|---|---|
| Polarisation | 3 dB | tumbling whip vs linear Yagi |
| Blockage | 3 dB | body, structure, terrain |
| Fading | 6 dB | multipath |
| **Total** | **12 dB** | |

### Step 5 — margin

```
Gross margin = P_rx − S
Net margin   = Gross − 12 dB
```

**Net margin > 10 dB = the link closes.**

---

## Worked example 1 — flight, 2 km, with the Yagi

```
FSPL = 32.45 + 20log₁₀(2) + 20log₁₀(867) = 97.2 dB

TX power                      +14.00 dBm
CanSat antenna                 +3.58 dBi
CanSat feed                    −0.60 dB
Free space path loss          −97.23 dB
Ground Yagi                   +11.00 dBi
Ground feed                    −2.10 dB
                              ──────────
Received power                −71.35 dBm
Sensitivity                  −132.00 dBm
                              ──────────
Gross link margin              60.65 dB
Impairments                   −12.00 dB
                              ──────────
NET LINK MARGIN                48.65 dB   ✅
```

## Worked example 2 — your ground test, 2 km, rubber ducky, +10 dBm, 1.5 m / 3 m

```
PL = 40log₁₀(2000) − 20log₁₀(1.5) − 20log₁₀(3)
   = 132.04 − 3.52 − 9.54 = 118.98 dB

TX power                      +10.00 dBm
CanSat antenna                 +2.00 dBi
CanSat feed                    −0.60 dB
Two-ray path loss            −118.98 dB
Ground antenna                 +2.00 dBi
Ground feed                    −2.10 dB
                              ──────────
Received power               −107.68 dBm
Sensitivity                  −132.00 dBm
                              ──────────
Gross link margin              24.32 dB
Impairments                   −12.00 dB
                              ──────────
NET LINK MARGIN                12.32 dB   ✅
```

Note the contrast: **48.65 dB in flight vs 12.32 dB on the ground at the same distance.** That 36 dB is the two-ray penalty. Your ground test is the hard case.

## Corrected field sheet — rubber ducky both ends, +10 dBm

| Distance | h = 1.5/1.5 m | h = 1.5/3 m | h = 2/6 m |
|---|---|---|---|
| 500 m | −89.6 dBm | −83.6 dBm | −75.1 dBm |
| 1 km | −101.6 dBm | −95.6 dBm | −87.1 dBm |
| 1.5 km | −108.7 dBm | −102.7 dBm | −94.2 dBm |
| **2 km** | **−113.7 dBm** | **−107.7 dBm** | **−99.2 dBm** |
| **Net margin @ 2 km** | **6.3 dB** ~ | **12.3 dB** ✅ | **20.8 dB** ✅ |

**Get the ground station to 3 m minimum. 6 m and 2 km is comfortable.**

## Closing the loop — validate prediction against measurement

This is the part that makes it a CDR result rather than an arithmetic exercise. At each distance, record measured RSSI and compare to predicted:

- **Within ±6 dB** — model validated, quote it with confidence
- **Measured 10–15 dB low** — usually antenna height or an obstruction you didn't count
- **Measured 25+ dB low** — a fault, not propagation (this is what your dual band antenna is doing)

Present the comparison as a table in the CDR. A measured curve tracking a predicted curve is far stronger evidence than either alone.

---

I can write this as a small Python calculator in `cdr_tests/` — you'd give it distance, power, antenna gains and heights, and it prints the full budget table plus the measured-vs-predicted comparison from your logged CSV. That's reusable for the CDR document and for every test run after this. Want it? I can do that alongside the `MARK` and PER-summary additions to `test_rf_rx.ino` I offered earlier.

**CLAUDE** (2026-08-08T05:23:31)

Both sketches rewritten. Here's what changed and how to use them.

## What's different

**The packet is now the real one.** The old sketch sent a filler string. Airtime — and therefore error rate at a given signal level — depends on payload length, so a wrong-sized test packet measures a link you aren't flying. `buildPacket()` now emits the exact layout from `Config.h` §6:

```
<TEAM>,<TIME_S>,<PKT>,<ALT_M>,<PRES_PA>,<TEMP_C>,<BUS_V>,<GNSS_TIME>,
<LAT>,<LON>,<GPS_ALT>,<SATS>,<ACC_X>,<ACC_Y>,<ACC_Z>,<SPIN>,<STATE>,
<FLAGS>,F0,<txPower>\r\n
```

Twenty fields, base-36, `\r\n` terminated, padded in the optional-data region to the 99-byte flight wall. Values are synthetic; **the length and field structure are real**, and those are what decide airtime.

At SF10/BW125/CR4-5, 99 bytes = **985 ms** airtime in a 1000 ms slot. That's why `RxEveryNCycles 3` is now in there — a 400 ms uplink window cannot open every cycle, exactly as `Config.h` does it with `RX_EVERY_N_CYCLES`.

**Transmit power rides in the packet** as the `F0` optional field. The ground station can't close a budget without it, and reading it off a second serial monitor 2 km away isn't a plan.

I did **not** copy `Base36.h` into `cdr_tests/`. A third copy is a third thing that can drift out of step with your schema sum. Both sketches carry a few lines of test-only base-36 scaffolding instead, clearly marked as not the flight codec.

## Setting up the budget

Type these once on the RX before you walk out:

```
GTX 3.58     CanSat antenna gain, dBi
LTX 0.6      CanSat feed loss, dB
GRX 2.0      ground antenna gain (11.0 when the Yagi arrives)
LRX 2.1      ground feed loss
HT 1.5       CanSat antenna height, m
HR 3.0       ground antenna height, m
IMP 12       impairment allowance
```

Defaults are already the `COMPLIANCE_AUDIT.md` §3.2 values. Then at each stop:

```
MARK 500
```

That sets the distance and resets the rolling window so the next summary covers only that position.

## What you get per packet

```
Packet 47 arrived. RSSI -95.6 dBm, SNR 8.2 dB, sent at 10 dBm.
  Margin 36.4 dB on RSSI and 23.2 dB on SNR.
  At 1000 m the budget predicts -95.6 dBm by two ray, so the link is
  running +0.0 dB against prediction.
```

Plus a 20-column CSV row carrying measured, predicted, the delta, path loss, which model was used, packet bytes, airtime, live PER, and all the budget inputs — so a log is self-describing months later when you're writing the CDR.

## The two summaries

Every 30 seconds (or on `SUMMARY`):

```
SUMMARY at 1000 metres
  Received 29 of 30 expected, lost 1, packet error rate 3.3 percent.
  RSSI mean -95.4, worst -97.0, best -94.0 dBm.
  Worst link margin 35.0 dB on RSSI, 22.6 dB on SNR.
  Verdict: good, this distance passes
```

It reports **worst-case** margin, not mean — a mean hides exactly the packets that were about to be lost. Verdict thresholds are your ≥95% criterion.

`BUDGET` prints the full CDR-format table on demand, ending with:

```
  Measured minus predicted    +0.30 dB   the model is validated
```

Bands: within ±6 dB validated, 10–15 dB check height and obstructions, 25+ dB **that's a fault, not propagation** — which is the band your dual band antenna was sitting in.

## Two things worth knowing

**Path model selection is automatic.** It computes free-space and two-ray and takes whichever predicts more loss, naming it in every row. Breakpoint is `4π·h_t·h_r/λ` — the version where the two models actually cross. That's the definition I got wrong in my earlier hand calculation, and it's why my field-sheet numbers were 10 dB pessimistic. The code uses the correct one.

**The TX side now reports the reverse link.** When it hears an uplink it prints that RSSI, SNR and margin. That's the half of the budget your ground station physically cannot see, measured at the harder end — small antenna, no mast. Read it off the Teensy monitor when you're back.

Nothing in `vritra_fc/` or `vritra_gs/` was touched. No wire format, no radio parameter, no schema sum. I only updated the two `cdr_tests/README.md` rows.

**Both sketches are written and unverified** — there's no C++ toolchain here, so I've checked structure, pin agreement and register values against `Radio.cpp` and `vritra_gs.ino`, but neither has been compiled. Build them in the IDE before you trust a reading, and shout if anything doesn't compile.

**CLAUDE** (2026-08-08T05:31:18)

Both rewritten. Plain code, plain output, no calculations done for you.

## What the output looks like now

**Transmitter:**

```
Packet 12
  Content  025,C,C,4M4,25MM,8A,VQ,15G0,1SBHK,4HB9L,1VQ,9,E8,E1,152,BS,3,2Z,F0,A,XXXXXXXXXXXXXXXXXXXX
  Bytes    99
  Power    10 dBm
  Airtime  985 ms
```

**Receiver:**

```
Packet 12
  Content  025,C,C,4M4,25MM,8A,VQ,15G0,1SBHK,4HB9L,1VQ,9,E8,E1,152,BS,3,2Z,F0,A,XXXXXXXXXXXXXXXXXXXX
  Bytes    99
  RSSI     -95.6 dBm
  SNR      8.2 dB
  Power    10 dBm
  Received 12
  Lost     0
```

That's every number you need. Sensitivity is −132 dBm at SF10, SNR threshold is −15 dB, so `margin = RSSI − (−132)` and `SNR margin = SNR − (−15)`.

## Changes made

- **All budget maths removed** from the receiver — no predicted RSSI, no path loss models, no `MARK`/`GTX`/`HT` commands, no CSV block.
- **Serial output humanised.** No underscores, no hyphens joining words, no box borders. Minus signs only where a number is genuinely negative.
- **Commands are plain words** — `TX ON`, `TX OFF`, `CALIBRATE`, `STATUS`, `HELP`. The receiver still silently accepts the `CMD_` forms so your ground control app keeps working, but nothing with an underscore is ever printed or shown in the help.
- **Code simplified** — plain `#define` state numbers instead of enums, short comments, one job per function, no clever formatting.

## One bug I caught and fixed

The padding that brings the packet up to 99 bytes was being appended straight onto the transmit power value. `X` is a valid base-36 digit, so the receiver would have decoded `A` (10 dBm) as `AXXXXXXXX…` — a wildly wrong number, silently. The padding now starts with a comma so it becomes its own field and can't touch the power.

## What was kept, deliberately

These look like clutter but each one is a real failure:

- `setMISO`/`setMOSI`/`setSCK` before `SPI1.begin()` — wrong order gives error 707
- `setRfSwitchTable()` called twice — `begin()` wipes it
- `setOutputPower(power, true)` — the module only wires the high power path
- `calibrateImageRejection(863, 870)` — the chip calibrates at 915 MHz and goes quietly deaf at 867 without this
- Only one interrupt on the ESP32, transmit finished by polling with `delay(1)` — a second interrupt crashed the board
- Brownout register disabled, 250 ms settle, 30 ms after transmit — the supply dip that was rebooting the ESP32

The packet is still the real 99-byte layout, so airtime and error rate match what you'll actually fly.

**Both files are written and unverified** — no C++ toolchain here, so I've checked structure, pins and register values against `Radio.cpp` and `vritra_gs.ino`, but neither has been compiled. Build them and tell me if anything errors.

**CLAUDE** (2026-08-08T05:41:03)

Good news first: **zero packets lost across 22 packets, SNR steady at 11.8 to 13.5 dB.** That's a clean, healthy link — no packet errors, comfortably above the −15 dB SF10 threshold on every single packet. Whatever was causing the earlier 30 dB anomaly is not showing up here. The dual band antenna on `LORA_ANT` with a good connector is working.

But don't read anything into the **RSSI** number at 1 metre. −80 dBm is not what you'd expect from Friis at 1 m with two ~3.5 dBi antennas and 10 dBm out — that formula would predict something like −15 dBm, not −80. The gap isn't a fault this time, it's that **1 m is the wrong distance to test at**:

- **You're inside the near field, right at its edge.** Free space loss (Friis) assumes far-field propagation. At 867 MHz with these antennas the far-field boundary is only centimetres away, so 1 m is technically far field, but multipath at that range dominates everything.
- **Indoor multipath at short range is brutal.** Reflections off the table, your hands, walls a metre away arrive with almost the same delay as the direct path and can cancel it by 20 to 40 dB. This is a deep fade, not a broken antenna.
- **Polarisation and orientation matter enormously up close.** If the two whips weren't both vertical and pointing the same way, that alone costs 10 to 30 dB at this range.
- **It is not receiver saturation.** If the front end were overloaded, RSSI would clip near −20 to −30 dBm, not drop to −80. A saturation artifact pushes the number up toward the ceiling, not down. So −80 dBm is a real measured loss from something in the environment, not a radio-side artifact.

**What this test actually tells you:** the antenna, connector, port and both sketches are working correctly. What it can't tell you is anything about actual antenna gain or real-world range, because 1 m indoors is dominated by fading, not free-space loss.

**Next step:** move to a real distance — even 20 to 30 m outdoors, clear line of sight, both whips held vertical and away from your body or any metal — and the RSSI numbers will start meaning something you can compare against the −78.8 dBm predicted at 2 km in `HANDOFF.md`, or against the rubber ducky's earlier numbers. That's also the point where a genuine antenna problem (if there still is one) would show up as an RSSI gap rather than being swamped by multipath.
